I don't see myself as dangerously thin, but I have this constant, intense fear of ballooning up if I don't keep a tight grip on my weight.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself eating a large amount of food in a short period, feeling like you can't stop, and then feeling guilty or panicked afterwards, leading to purging or excessive exercise?\n\nPatient: No, nothing like that at all. My struggle is the exact opposite—I strictly limit what I eat and try to avoid food as much as possible. Even the thought of having a normal portion, like a burger or a second helping, panics me, so I'd never eat a large amount of food.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your menstrual cycle, such as irregular periods or cessation of menstruation, since you started strictly controlling your food intake?\n\nPatient: Actually, I'm a man, so I don't have a menstrual cycle. I think there might be a slight mix-up with my file on that one.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant physical symptoms such as hair loss, dry skin, or feeling cold most of the time since you started restricting your food intake?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I definitely feel cold all the time, and I’ve even passed out at the shop once or twice, but I honestly think my wife and the doctors are overreacting about it. To me, those things just show how disciplined I'm being and how seriously I'm taking my health.

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see us today?\n\nPatient: To be honest, I'm mainly here because my wife and family insisted on it. I just can't shake this constant fear of putting on weight, so I keep cutting back on what I eat even though they keep telling me I'm already too thin.\n\nDoctor: Do you often check your weight or look in the mirror to check for signs of weight gain or fatness?\n\nPatient: Yes, I find myself stepping on the scale multiple times a day because I get this rush of panic at the thought of gaining any weight. If the number goes up even by half a pound, I immediately cut back on what I eat to make sure I don't balloon up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is normal, or do you see yourself as overweight even when others tell you that you are underweight?\n\nPatient: My wife and doctors keep telling me I'm too skinny, but I honestly think they're overreacting and that I'm just being disciplined and healthy. I don't see myself as dangerously thin, but I have this constant, intense fear of ballooning up if I don't keep a tight grip on my weight.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself eating a large amount of food in a short period, feeling like you can't stop, and then feeling guilty or panicked afterwards, leading to purging or excessive exercise?\n\nPatient: No, nothing like that at all. My struggle is the exact opposite—I strictly limit what I eat and try to avoid food as much as possible. Even the thought of having a normal portion, like a burger or a second helping, panics me, so I'd never eat a large amount of food.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your menstrual cycle, such as irregular periods or cessation of menstruation, since you started strictly controlling your food intake?\n\nPatient: Actually, I'm a man, so I don't have a menstrual cycle. I think there might be a slight mix-up with my file on that one.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant physical symptoms such as hair loss, dry skin, or feeling cold most of the time since you started restricting your food intake?\n\nPatient: I definitely feel cold all the time, and I’ve even passed out at the shop once or twice, but I honestly think my wife and the doctors are overreacting about it. To me, those things just show how disciplined I'm being and how seriously I'm taking my health.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your strict diet and weight control measures are necessary to prevent a catastrophic outcome, such as a serious health problem or loss of control over your eating, and that this fear drives your behaviors?\n\nPatient: I don't think my diet is causing any kind of health crisis—to me, it just feels like I'm taking my health and discipline seriously. But I do get an intense, sudden panic about ballooning up overnight if I eat a normal meal, and that fear is what drives me to constantly check the scale and cut back even more.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what specifically happens when you look in the mirror - do you see a fat person even when you know the scale says you're underweight, or is it more about the fear of what could happen if you don't control your weight?"

It's really about the terrifying fear of losing control and ballooning up overnight if I eat normally. Checking the scale constantly is how I manage that panic and keep myself in check.

Steven reluctantly agreed to a psychiatric evaluation when his wife confronted him with the extent of his weight loss and how it was affecting their home life.\n- Chief Complaint: I can’t shake this fear of putting on weight, so I keep cutting back on what I eat even though my family says I’m already too thin.\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S043 (Restrictive_Intake): Most days I just nurse a black coffee at breakfast, maybe pick at some lettuce or grilled chicken at lunch, then tell my wife I already ate at the shop so I can get away with just a tiny portion of plain vegetables at dinner, and even then I’ll cut the pieces smaller and push food around so it looks like I ate more than I did. \n- S044 (Low_Weight): My pants hang off me now and my wedding ring is loose, but instead of worrying about it I get a weird sense of pride when coworkers or guys at basketball say I look ‘shredded’ or ask how I got so lean. \n- S045 (Fear_of_Weight_Gain): If I even think about having a burger or a second helping, I get this rush of panic that I’ll balloon up overnight, so I find myself stepping on the scale multiple times a day and cutting back even more any time the number bumps up, even by half a pound. \n- S084 (Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition): My wife and the doctor keep telling me I’m too skinny and that passing out at the shop and feeling cold all the time is serious, but honestly it just feels like they’re overreacting to a few extra skipped meals when to me it just means I’m finally taking my health and discipline seriously. \n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: Lately I’m so drained and lightheaded from eating so little that I’ve had to leave cars unfinished at the shop and skip family outings, which has my boss quietly asking if I’m okay and my wife saying she feels like she’s doing everything at home without me.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. Do NOT reveal any other symptoms.\n- Chief complaint for the first turn: I can’t shake this fear of putting on weight, so I keep cutting back on what I eat even though my family says I’m already too thin.\n- Answer only what the doctor asks. Express the intense, immediate panic of 'ballooning up' and losing control if the scale goes up even by half a pound, while also explaining that staying lean feels like a sign of discipline and being healthy, rather than seeing it as a medical issue."

What brings you in to see us today?\n\nPatient: To be honest, I'm mainly here because my wife and family insisted on it. I just can't shake this constant fear of putting on weight, so I keep cutting back on what I eat even though they keep telling me I'm already too thin.\n\nDoctor: Do you often check your weight or look in the mirror to check for signs of weight gain or fatness?\n\nPatient: Yes, I find myself stepping on the scale multiple times a day because I get this rush of panic at the thought of gaining any weight. If the number goes up even by half a pound, I immediately cut back on what I eat to make sure I don't balloon up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is normal, or do you see yourself as overweight even when others tell you that you are underweight?\n\nPatient: My wife and doctors keep telling me I'm too skinny, but I honestly think they're overreacting and that I'm just being disciplined and healthy. I don't see myself as dangerously thin, but I have this constant, intense fear of ballooning up if I don't keep a tight grip on my weight.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself eating a large amount of food in a short period, feeling like you can't stop, and then feeling guilty or panicked afterwards, leading to purging or excessive exercise?\n\nPatient: No, nothing like that at all. My struggle is the exact opposite—I strictly limit what I eat and try to avoid food as much as possible. Even the thought of having a normal portion, like a burger or a second helping, panics me, so I'd never eat a large amount of food.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your menstrual cycle, such as irregular periods or cessation of menstruation, since you started strictly controlling your food intake?\n\nPatient: Actually, I'm a man, so I don't have a menstrual cycle. I think there might be a slight mix-up with my file on that one.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant physical symptoms such as hair loss, dry skin, or feeling cold most of the time since you started restricting your food intake?\n\nPatient: I definitely feel cold all the time, and I’ve even passed out at the shop once or twice, but I honestly think my wife and the doctors are overreacting about it. To me, those things just show how disciplined I'm being and how seriously I'm taking my health.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your strict diet and weight control measures are necessary to prevent a catastrophic outcome, such as a serious health problem or loss of control over your eating, and that this fear drives your behaviors?\n\nPatient: I don't think my diet is causing any kind of health crisis—to me, it just feels like I'm taking my health and discipline seriously. But I do get an intense, sudden panic about ballooning up overnight if I eat a normal meal, and that fear is what drives me to constantly check the scale and cut back even more.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what specifically happens when you look in the mirror - do you see a fat person even when you know the scale says you're underweight, or is it more about the fear of what could happen if you don't control your weight?\n\nPatient: I don't see a fat person in the mirror; I actually feel quite proud of how lean and \"shredded\" I look, even if others think I'm too thin. It's really about the terrifying fear of losing control and ballooning up overnight if I eat normally. Checking the scale constantly is how I manage that panic and keep myself in check.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your body and weight, is it the actual appearance that worries you, or is it more about the potential consequences, like health problems or loss of control, if you were to gain weight?"

It's really a combination of both. I get this intense, immediate panic of losing control and ballooning up if the scale goes up by even half a pound. At the same time, staying this lean feels like a true sign of discipline and being healthy, rather than some kind of medical issue.

It's really about the terrifying fear of losing control and ballooning up overnight if I eat normally. Checking the scale constantly is how I manage that panic and keep myself in check.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your body and weight, is it the actual appearance that worries you, or is it more about the potential consequences, like health problems or loss of control, if you were to gain weight?\n\nPatient: It's really a combination of both. I get this intense, immediate panic of losing control and ballooning up if the scale goes up by even half a pound. At the same time, staying this lean feels like a true sign of discipline and being healthy, rather than some kind of medical issue.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe a situation where you would feel comfortable not checking your weight or restricting your food intake, or do you feel like you always need to be in control of your eating to prevent weight gain?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
To be honest, there really isn't any situation where I would feel comfortable letting go of this control. I feel a constant, driving need to stay in control of my eating and weight to avoid gaining even a fraction of a pound. Even just the thought of relaxing my rules brings on a rush of panic about ballooning up overnight, which is why I feel compelled to constantly step on the scale and restrict my food to maintain my discipline.

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.
